Unitary dual and matrix coefficients of compact nilpotent p p -adic Lie groups with dimension d ≤ 5 d\leq 5 Thanks: The author is supported by the grace of God, a.k.a. The Truth, and the magnanimity of his majesty Lord Bruce the First, ruler of The Lands Between
J.P. Velasquez-Rodriguez

Abstract
. Let p > 2 p>2 be a prime number, and let 𝔾 \mathbb{G} be a compact nilpotent p p -adic Lie group with nilpotency class 𝒩 < p \mathscr{N}<p . In this note we calculate explicitly the unitary dual and the matrix coefficients of every compact nilpotent p p -adic Lie group with dimension less or equal than 5 5 . As an application, we provide the corresponding spectral theorem for the Vladimirov sub-Laplacian, and show how this operator provides a non-trivial example of a globally hypoelliptic operator on compact nilpotent p p -adic Lie groups.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary; 22E35, 58J40 ; Secondary: 20G05, 35R03, 42A16.

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Motivation

Noncommutative groups have been very important mathematical objects since their introduction, specially after physicist started viewing them as the appropriate language to talk about
transformations and symmetries of physical systems
. For instance, Marius Sophus Lie initially introduced what we call now
Lie groups
to study continuous symmetries related to differential equations. He aimed to understand how these symmetries could be represented, which led to significant advancements in both mathematics and physics. Lie groups allow for the analysis of transformations that preserve the structure of differential equations, enabling the classification and solution of these equations through the concept of symmetry. By applying the tools of differential calculus to groups, Lie could explore how these symmetries interact and how they could simplify complex problems.
z
The archetypal example of the principle Lie discovered is the
heat equation
on the interval
[0,1]
, which can be thought as a bar of a certain material with length one, and with periodic boundary conditions:
f(0)=f(1)
. This equation was fully solved by Joseph Fourier in his seminal work
Théorie Analytique de la Chaleur

[
14
]
, and it marks a pivotal moment in the history of both mathematics and physics, as this groundbreaking book presents the first comprehensive mathematical treatment of solutions to the heat diffusion equation

\frac{\partial f}{\partial t}=\mathscr{L}f,\quad\mathscr{L}:=\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}},

where the operator
\mathscr{L}
is the one-dimensional Laplacian. This equation serves as a fundamental model for understanding how heat disperses through a given medium, and Fourier’s analysis introduces a variety of special series and integrals to articulate its solutions. The implications of Fourier’s work were profound; he established that real-valued functions, which can be interpreted as representing heat distributions across a physical object, could be decomposed into sums of simpler, more manageable functions: specifically, sines and cosines. These functions turn out to be a complete system of eigenfunctions of the operator
\mathscr{L}
, which means they can serve as the fundamental building blocks for constructing more complicated functions. Fourier’s concept extends naturally into the realm of complex-valued functions, which can similarly be written as sums of complex exponentials
\mathscr{e}_{k}(\theta)=e^{2\pi ik\theta}
, where
k
is an integer number. This way of expressing functions is not merely an isolated case; it exemplifies a broader mathematical phenomenon that would not be fully understood until centuries later, particularly due to the advancements made in group theory and the study of topological vector spaces.

From a more elaborated point of view, functions defined on
[0,1]
with periodic boundary conditions can be identified with functions on the unit circle

\mathbb{T}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:zz^{*}=1\},

where
z^{*}
indicates the complex conjugate of
z
. This set is actually a topological group with a differential structure, i.e., a Lie group, and its algebraic structure is important because, by the Peter-Weyl theorem, the set

\widehat{\mathbb{T}}:=\{\mathscr{e}_{k}:k\in\mathbb{Z}\},

which is a complete collection of representatives of equivalence classes of unitary irreducible representations of
\mathbb{T}
, serves as an orthonormal basis for the space
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})\cong L^{2}[0,1]
. In particular, if we only consider real-valued functions, we recover Fourier’s original ideas. But if instead we choose to consider more general noncommutative spaces, the extensions and generalizations of Fourier’s techniques are collectively referred to as
Fourier analysis
. This field encompasses a great deal of examples where functions defined on certain topological spaces can be decomposed into sums of ”simpler” functions. When one focuses on spaces endowed with additional algebraic structure, which are spaces rich in symmetries, there are two distinct pathways for exploration:

(1)

Studying locally connected spaces:
A natural progression from the theory on the torus
\mathbb{T}
involves investigating compact Lie groups. This topic is meticulously addressed in the work of
[
26
]
, which lays out the foundational elements of a comprehensive theory of pseudo-differential operators applicable to compact Lie groups. Understanding the representation theory of these groups is a crucial aspect, though it often presents significant challenges. The complexity of the representation theory can vary greatly depending on the specific group in question. For example, the representation theory for
\mathrm{SU}(2)
is explored in
[
26
]
, while spin groups are discussed in
[
9
]
.

(2)

Studying totally disconnected spaces:
In the domain of totally disconnected spaces, a rich and intricate theory has developed concerning Fourier analysis on non-Archimedean local fields and their corresponding rings of integers. The first major generalization in this context involves examining what are known as
locally compact abelian Vilenkin groups
. These groups are characterized by specific sequences of compact open subgroups, and further details can be found in references such as
[
27
]
. Much like the locally connected case, when transitioning to non-commutative groups, we must consider compact
p
-adic Lie groups. These groups have predominantly been analyzed from an algebraic perspective within the literature
[
4
,
16
]
, as well as within the broader category of
compact Vilenkin groups
.

In the present paper, we choose to pursue the second pathway, initiating our exploration with compact nilpotent
p
-adic Lie groups. Over the past fifteen years, a comprehensive symbol calculus for compact (noncommutative) groups has been developed by researchers such as Ruzhansky, Turunen, and Wirth. Although their primary focus has been on compact Lie groups over the reals
[
26
]
, this new framework serves as a noncommutative extension of the classical Kohn–Nirenberg quantization. This quantization is an essential tool for analyzing differential operators on groups and presents several advantages compared to Hörmander’s principal calculus or other local methods. For a given Lie group
G
, this innovative approach leverages the representation theory and the corresponding harmonic analysis to establish a global Fourier transform, which effectively diagonalizes differential operators, including the Laplace-Beltrami operator. This is, in essence, a far-reaching generalization of the original ideas proposed by Fourier.

Our motivation to extend the ideas known on real Lie groups to the t.d. case is deeply rooted in a principle attributed to Harish-Chandra, known as the ”Lefschetz principle.” This principle asserts that
real groups,
p
-adic groups, and automorphic forms—corresponding to both archimedean and non-archimedean local fields, as well as number fields—should be regarded on equal footing, and that ideas and results emerging from one of these categories should be transferable to the other two

[
21
]
. Within this framework, we aim to extend certain techniques from harmonic analysis on real groups to the realm of compact
p
-adic Lie groups. These topological groups have primarily been examined in the literature through an algebraic lens. Our main focus will be on Fourier analysis, particularly investigating the Vladimirov-Taibleson operator, which has a significant parallel with the fractional Laplacian in real analysis, within the context of non-commutative groups defined over the
p
-adic integers.

To further elucidate what we mean by the phrase
the extension of some techniques from harmonic analysis on Lie groups
, it is crucial to understand that analysis on Lie groups fundamentally relies on the differential structure of the group as a smooth manifold. To date, to the best of the author’s knowledge, there exists no comprehensive theory of derivative, differential, or pseudo-differential operators specifically on
p
-adic manifolds, nor on the broader class of non-commutative locally profinite groups. Despite the rich theoretical landscape surrounding
p
-adic Lie groups, algebraic groups, and
\mathbb{K}
-analytic manifolds, significant gaps remain in our understanding. In recent years, however, researchers like P.E. Bradley and collaborators have begun the study of integral operators operators (which can be regarded as pseudo-differential) that remain invariant under finite group actions, heat equations on Mumford curves, and
p
-adic Laplacians
[
5
,
6
,
7
]
. Beyond this, it is worth noting that only Kochubei has examined equations defined on a
p
-adic ball
[
19
]
, and a limited number of works have addressed general ultrametric spaces
[
2
]
. A common thread that emerges within the
p
-adic side of the theory is the Vladimirov–Taibleson operator, which provides a definition of “differentiability” for functions defined on ultrametric spaces. Most pseudo-differential equations in the literature, especially those aimed at real-world applications, are expressed in terms of this operator or a similar one
[
17
]
. This operator is commonly regarded as a kind of fractional Laplacian for complex-valued functions on totally disconnected spaces
[
20
]
, particularly because, in some cases, it actually coincides with the isotropic Laplacian associated with a certain ultrametric in general ultrametric spaces, as demonstrated by the works of A. Bendikov, A. Grigoryan, C. Pittet, and W. Woess.

Definition 1.1
.

•

We will use the symbols
a\lesssim b
and
a\gtrsim b
to indicate that the quantity
a
is, respectively, less or equal, or greater or equal than a constant times the quantity
b
. We use the notation
a\asymp b
to indicate that
a\lesssim b
and
a\gtrsim b
hold simultaneously.

•

Let
(G,\mu)
be a measure space. For a measurable set
A\subset G
, we will denote by
\mu(A)
the measure of the set
A
, and the symbol
\mathds{1}_{A}
will be used to denote the indicator function on
A
.

•

Let
G
be a compact topological group. We will denote by
\mathrm{Rep}(G)
the collection of all (equivalent classes of) continuous, unitary, finite-dimensional representations of
G
. The symbol
\widehat{G}
will be used to denote the
unitary dual
of
G
, that is, the collection of all (equivalence classes of) continuous unitary irreducible representations of
G
. Remember how any element
[\pi]
of
\mathrm{Rep}(G)
can be written as a direct sum of finitely many elements of
\widehat{G}
.

1.2.
Our setting

This paper focuses on the representation theory of compact nilpotent
p
-adic Lie groups with dimensions up to 5. In his seminal work
[
11
]
, Dixmier established that any nilpotent Lie algebra with dimension
d\leq 5
is isomorphic to one of the following:

•

Dimension 1:

\mathfrak{g}_{1}
.

•

Dimension 2:

(\mathfrak{g}_{1})^{2}
.

•

Dimension 3:

(\mathfrak{g}_{1})^{3}
,
\mathfrak{h}_{1}
.

•

Dimension 4:

(\mathfrak{g}_{1})^{4}
,
\mathfrak{h}_{1}\times\mathfrak{g}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{b}_{4}
.

•

Dimension 5:

(\mathfrak{g}_{1})^{5}
,
\mathfrak{h}_{1}\times(\mathfrak{g}_{1})^{2}
,
\mathfrak{b}_{4}\times\mathfrak{g}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{5,1}=\mathfrak{h}_{2}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{5,2}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{5,3}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{5,4}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{5,5}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{5,6}
.

The above listed Lie algebras are defined by the following commutation relations:

•

Dimension 3:

\mathfrak{h}_{1}\,:\quad[X_{1},X_{2}]=[X_{3}].

•

Dimension 4:

\mathfrak{b}_{4}\,:\quad[X_{1},X_{2}]=X_{3},\quad[X_{1},X_{3}]=X_{4}.

•

Dimension 5:

\mathfrak{g}^{5,1}=\mathfrak{h}_{2}\,:\quad[X_{1},X_{3}]=X_{5},\quad[X_{2},X_{4}]=X_{5}.

\mathfrak{g}^{5,2}\,:[X_{1},X_{2}]=X_{4},\quad[X_{1},X_{3}]=X_{5}.

\mathfrak{g}^{5,3}\,:[X_{1},X_{2}]=X_{4},\quad[X_{1},X_{4}]=X_{5},\quad[X_{2},X_{3}]=X_{5}.

\mathfrak{g}^{5,4}\,:[X_{1},X_{2}]=X_{3},\quad[X_{1},X_{3}]=X_{4},\quad[X_{2},X_{3}]=X_{5}.

\mathfrak{g}^{5,5}\,:[X_{1},X_{2}]=X_{3},\quad[X_{1},X_{3}]=X_{4},\quad[X_{1},X_{4}]=X_{5}.

\mathfrak{g}^{5,6}\,:[X_{1},X_{2}]=X_{3},\quad[X_{1},X_{3}]=X_{4},\quad[X_{1},X_{4}]=X_{5},\quad[X_{2},X_{3}]=X_{5}.

In his paper, Dixmier characterized the unitary dual of all Lie groups over
\mathbb{R}
that can be represented as the exponential image of the aforementioned Lie algebras, see
[
11
]
. Here in this paper, we aim to achieve a similar analysis for the compact
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-Lie modules derived from any of the specified commutation relations, including the case of
\mathfrak{g}^{5,4}
, which is referred to as the
Cartan algebra
by some authors. We have already begun this endeavor by explicitly calculating the unitary dual of the Heisenberg groups
\mathbb{H}_{d}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
,
d\in\mathbb{N}
, and the Engel group
\mathcal{B}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, which are the exponential images of the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-Lie modules
\mathfrak{h}_{d}
and
\mathfrak{b}_{4}
, respectively. See
[
28
,
29
]
for the full calculations. In this paper, we focus exclusively on the
5
-dimensional algebras
\mathfrak{g}^{5,i}
, where
2\leq i\leq 6
, and the unitary irreducible representations of the compact groups obtained as their exponential images. Our main objective is to derive explicit expressions for the unitary irreducible representations and the matrix coefficients of
5
-dimensional compact nilpotent
p
-adic Lie groups, which will later aid in the study of pseudo-differential operators. In particular, we will focus on a specific operator known as
the Vladimirov sub-Laplacian
, which we introduced in
[
29
,
28
]
. This operator shares some similarities with the sub-Laplacian on graded Lie groups over
\mathbb{R}
, or it can also be considered close to a Hörmander’s operator.

Compact nilpotent
p
-adic Lie groups have been extensively studied from an algebraic standpoint, particularly within the framework of pro-
p
groups or general profinite groups. In this work, we treat them as the unit ball
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{d}
, endowed with a non-commutative operation denoted by ”
\star
”, which gives
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}
the structure of a non-commutative (compact) topological group. While we explore their algebraic properties to some extent, our primary focus here is the analysis on these groups, specially the use of representation theory to study harmonic analysis and pseudo-differential operators. For instance, to illustrate our approach, consider the
(2d+1)
-dimensional Heisenberg group over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, denoted as
\mathbb{H}_{d}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, which can be defined as
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2d+1}
equipped with the non-commutative operation

(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},z)\star(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime},z^{\prime}):=(\textbf{x}+\textbf{x}^{\prime},\textbf{y}+\textbf{y}^{\prime},z+z^{\prime}+\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{y}^{\prime}).

For this group, Theorem
1.3
was established in
[
29
]
, which is the following characterization of the
unitary dual
, that is, the collection of all (equivalence classes of) unitary irreducible representations of
\mathbb{H}_{d}
.

Remark 1.2
.

In this paper, we will identify each equivalence class
\lambda
in
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\cong\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, with its associated representative in the complete system of representatives

\{1\}\cup\big\{\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}p^{-k}\,:\,\,\text{only finitely many $\lambda_{k}$ are non-zero.}\big\}.

Similarly, every time we consider an element of the quotients
\mathbb{Q}_{p}/p^{-n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
it will be chosen from the complete system of representatives

\{1\}\cup\big\{\sum_{k=n+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}p^{-k}\,:\,\text{only finitely many $\lambda_{k}$ are non-zero}\big\}.

Also, given any
p
-adic number
\lambda
, we will denote by
\vartheta(\lambda)
the
p
-adic valuation of
\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
p>2
. Let
\mathbb{H}_{d}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, or simply
\mathbb{H}_{d}
for short, be the
(2d+1)
-dimensional Heisenberg group over the
p
-adic integers, and let us denote by
\vartheta:\mathbb{Q}_{p}\to\mathbb{Z}
the
p
-adic valuation over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Let us denote by
\widehat{\mathbb{H}}_{d}
the unitary dual of
\mathbb{H}_{d}
, i.e., the collection of all equivalence classes of unitary irreducible representations of
\mathbb{H}_{d}
. Then we can identify
\widehat{\mathbb{H}}_{d}
with the following subset of
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{2d+1}\cong\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{2d+1}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2d+1}
:

\widehat{\mathbb{H}}_{d}=\{(\xi,\eta,\lambda)\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{2d+1}\,:(\xi,\eta)\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{2d}/p^{\vartheta(\lambda)}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2d}\}.

Moreover, each non-trivial representation
[\pi_{(\xi,\eta,\lambda)}]\in\widehat{\mathbb{H}}_{d}
can be realized in the finite dimensional sub-space
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
of
L^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d})
defined as

\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}:=\spn_{\mathbb{C}}\{\varphi_{h}\,:\,h\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}/p^{-\vartheta(\lambda)}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}\},\,\,\,\varphi_{h}(u):=|\lambda|_{p}^{d/2}\mathds{1}_{h+{p^{-\vartheta(\lambda)}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}}(u),\,\,\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})=|\lambda|_{p}^{d},

where the representation acts on functions
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
according to the formula

\pi_{(\xi,\eta,\lambda)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},z)\varphi(u):=e^{2\pi i\{\xi\cdot\mathbf{x}+\eta\cdot\mathbf{y}+\lambda(z+u\cdot\mathbf{y})\}_{p}}\varphi(u+\mathbf{x}),\,\,\,\,\varphi\in\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}.

With this explicit realization, and by choosing the basis
\{\varphi_{h}\,:\,h\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}/{p^{-\vartheta(\lambda)}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}\}
for each representation space, the associated matrix coefficients are given by

(\pi_{(\xi,\eta,\lambda)})_{hh^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},z)=e^{2\pi i\{\xi\cdot\mathbf{x}+\eta\cdot\mathbf{y}+\lambda(z+h^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{y})\}_{p}}\mathds{1}_{h-h^{\prime}+{p^{-\vartheta(\lambda)}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}}(\mathbf{x}).

Sometimes we will use the notation

\mathcal{V}_{(\xi,\eta,\lambda)}:=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{(\pi_{(\xi,\eta,\lambda)})_{hh^{\prime}}\,:\,h,h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}/{p^{-\vartheta(\lambda)}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}\}.

The above result, whose statement is included here for a matter of completeness, is the model for the theorems we will be proving on
\mathbb{G}^{5,i}
,
2\leq i\leq 6
. Concretely, we are interested in showing how the unitary dual of any nilpotent
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-Lie group, with
d=5
, can be identified with some subset of
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{5}
, kind of like a pruned version of the three
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{5}
particular to each group. The guess is: the same holds true for higher dimensional groups, and one should be able to obtain the matrix coefficients with a similar procedure, but proving it is beyond the scope of this work.

1.3.
Main goals

In 1967, Hörmander proved that, under the assumption that the system of smooth vector fields
X_{1},\ldots,X_{\kappa}
generates the entire tangent space at any point, a condition nowadays called
Hörmander’s condition
, the operator

(1)

L:=\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa}X_{i}^{2}+X_{0}+c,

which is called a
Hörmander’s operator
, has the following property: if
Lu=f
and
f
is smooth, we can conclude for sure that
u
is also smooth. Operators satisfying this condition are called
hypoelliptic operators
, and they play a very important role in the theory of partial differential operators. Important examples of Hörmander’s operators are the sub-Laplacians on Lie groups, see
[
8
]
and the references therein.

In general, proving that a given partial differential operator is hypoelliptic is a non-trivial problem, and there is a substantial body of literature dedicated to addressing this question. In this paper we consider the problem of the global hypoellipticity on compact nilpotent groups over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Specifically, we will study the
directional Vladimirov–Taibleson operators
introduced in
[
29
,
28
]
, which somehow reassemble directional derivatives.

Definition 1.4
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a non-archimedean local field with ring of integers
\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}
, prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}=\textbf{p}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}
, and residue field
\mathbb{F}_{q}=\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}/\textbf{p}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}.
Let
\mathfrak{g}=\spn_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}}\{X_{1},..,X_{d}\}
be a nilpotent
\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}
-Lie algebra, and let
\mathbb{G}
be the exponential image of
\mathfrak{g}
, so that
\mathbb{G}
is a compact nilpotent
\mathbb{K}
-Lie group. We will use the symbol symbol
\partial_{X}^{\alpha}
to denote the
directional Vladimirov–Taibleson operator in the direction of
X\in\mathfrak{g}
, or directional VT operator for short, which we define as

\partial_{X}^{\alpha}f(\mathbf{x}):=\frac{1-q^{\alpha}}{1-q^{-(\alpha+1)}}\int_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}}\frac{f(\mathbf{x}\star\textbf{exp}(tX)^{-1})-f(\mathbf{x})dt}{|t|_{\mathbb{K}}^{\alpha+1}},\,\,\,\,f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G}).

Here
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G})
denotes the space of smooth functions on
\mathbb{G}
, i.e., the collection of locally constant functions with a fixed index of local constancy.

Remark 1.5
.

Notice how the above definition is independent of any coordinate system we choose for the group as a manifold. Even though we are using the exponential map in the definition, this does not mean we are using the exponential system of coordinates. First,
x\in\mathbb{G}
is not written in coordinates, and second, we know there is a one to one correspondence between elements of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and one-parameter subgroups of
\mathbb{G}
. Here a one-parameter subgroup of
\mathbb{G}
is an analytic homomorphism
\gamma_{X}:\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}\to\mathbb{G}
. Using this fact, given any
X\in\mathfrak{g}
, we can define the directional VT operator in the direction of
X\in\mathfrak{g}
via the formula

\partial_{X}^{\alpha}f(\mathbf{x}):=\frac{1-q^{\alpha}}{1-q^{-(\alpha+1)}}\int_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}}\frac{f(\mathbf{x}\star\gamma_{X}(t)^{-1})-f(\mathbf{x})dt}{|t|_{\mathbb{K}}^{\alpha+1}}.

Since
\mathbb{G}
is compact, we can identify it with a matrix group, where all one parameter subgroups have the form
\gamma_{X}(t)=e^{tX}
, which justifies our initial definition. More generally, we can write any analytic vector field on
\mathbb{G}
as

X(\mathbf{x}):=\sum_{j=1}^{d}c_{j}(\mathbf{x})X_{j},

where the coefficient functions
c_{j}
are analytic. For this vector field, the directional VT operator is defined in a similar way as

\partial_{X}^{\alpha}f(\mathbf{x}):=\frac{1-q^{\alpha}}{1-q^{-(\alpha+1)}}\int_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}}\frac{f(\mathbf{x}\star\mathbf{exp}(tX(\mathbf{x}))^{-1})-f(\mathbf{x})dt}{|t|_{\mathbb{K}}^{\alpha+1}}.

However, in this work we wont consider the case where the coefficients are not constant, because such operators are not necessarily invariant.

By using Definition
1.4
, we can link an specific Vladimirov-type operator to each direction
X\in\mathfrak{g}
and subsequently study the resulting operators. While this association does not preserve the Lie algebra structure, as seen in Lie groups over the real numbers, the resulting operators are nonetheless interesting and share similarities with partial differential operators on Lie groups. We want to study polynomials in these operators and, specifically, a distinguished one called here the
Vladimirov sub-Laplacian
, which we can understand very well in the nilpotent case,
d:=dim_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{G})\leq 5
, due to our explicit knowledge of the representation theory. For this operator, which we define in principle for compact nilpotent groups, we would like to pose the following conjecture:

Conjecture
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a non-archimedean local field with ring of integers
\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}
, prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}=\textbf{p}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}
, and residue field
\mathbb{F}_{q}=\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}/\textbf{p}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}.
Let
\mathfrak{g}=\spn_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}}\{X_{1},..,X_{d}\}
be a nilpotent
\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}
-Lie algebra, and let
\mathbb{G}
be the exponential image of
\mathfrak{g}
, so that
G
is a compact nilpotent
\mathbb{K}
-Lie group. Let
X_{1},...,X_{\kappa}
,
1\leq\kappa\leq d
, be a basis for
\mathfrak{g}/[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]
, so that when
\mathfrak{g}
is graded or stratified
X_{1},...,X_{\kappa}
generates
\mathfrak{g}
, and let
\mathbb{G}
be the exponential image of
\mathfrak{g}
. Then the
Vladimirov sub-Laplacian of order
\alpha>0
is a hypoelliptic operator on
G
, and it is invertible in the space of mean-zero functions. Here the Vladimirov sub-Laplacian is the pseudo-differential operator
\mathscr{L}^{\alpha}_{sub}
, defined on the space of smooth functions
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G})
via the formula

\mathscr{L}^{\alpha}_{sub}f(\mathbf{x}):=\sum_{k=1}^{\kappa}\partial_{X_{k}}^{\alpha}f(\mathbf{x}),

where
X_{1},...,X_{\kappa}
spans
\mathfrak{g}/[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]
.

Remark 1.6
.

To the knowledge of the author, an operator like the Vladimirov sub-Laplacian has not appeared before in the mathematical literature. The closest thing is probably the study of the
Vladimirov-Laplacian
, which the reader can find in the work of Rajkumar and Weisbart
[
24
]
, and the paper by Bendikov, Grigoryan, Pittet, and Woess
[
2
]
where the authors studied the operators

\mathscr{L}^{\alpha}f(\mathbf{x}):=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\partial_{X_{k}}^{\alpha}f(\mathbf{x}),

as the generators of a Dirichlet form associated with a certain jump kernel. See
[
2
, Section 5]
for more details. From the perspective of
[
24
]
, when
X_{k}=\mathbf{e}_{k}
are the canonical directions, the Vladimirov Laplacian is the infinitesimal generator of a certain Markov process called there
the product process
. Here we want to to emphasize how it is not necessary to restrict ourselves to the canonical directions, and furthermore we can extend the same analysis to noncommutative groups.

So, drawing an analogy between the real case and the non-archimedean case, we can think of the Vladimirov sub-Laplacian as an analog of the sub-Laplacian or Hörmander’s sum of squares for the non-archimedean case. Consequently, it is reasonable to expect the global hypoellipticity for these operators, and the second main goal of this paper is to demonstrate that this is indeed the case for all the compact nilpotent
p
-adic Lie groups, assuming the dimension of the group is less or equal than
5
, and the nilpotency index of the group is strictly less than
p
. As the main results of this paper, we will provide a complete description of the unitary dual and the matrix coefficients of the representations of these groups, and as an application we will obtain the spectal theorem for the Vladimirov sub-Laplacian on any of these groups. To be more precise, our goal in this work is to establish the following result, which justify our conjecture about sub-Laplacians on more general stratified, or even just nilpotent groups.

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a compact nilpotent
p
-adic Lie group with nilpotency class
\mathscr{N}
, and assume that
p>\mathscr{N}
,
d:=\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{G})\leq 5
. Let
\mathfrak{g}
be its associated
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-Lie module, and let let
\{X_{1},...,X_{\kappa}\}
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-basis for
\mathfrak{g}/[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]
. Then:

(1)

The unitary dual of
\mathbb{G}
can be indexed by a certain
\Gamma\subset\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{d}
, i.e. any
[\pi]\in\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
is equivalent to some
\pi_{\xi}
, with
\xi\in\Gamma
, and the annihilators

\widehat{\mathbb{G}}\cap\mathbb{G}(p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\bot}:=\{[\pi]\in\widehat{\mathbb{G}}\,:\,\pi|_{\mathbb{G}(p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p})}=I_{d_{\pi}}\},

can be identified with the balls

B_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}(n):=\{\xi\in\Gamma\,:\,\|\xi\|_{p}\leq p^{n}\}.

(2)

The Vladimirov sub-Laplacian, here defined as

\mathcal{L}_{sub}^{\alpha}f(\mathbf{x}):=\sum_{k=1}^{\kappa}\partial_{X_{k}}^{\alpha}f(\mathbf{x}),\quad f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G}),

is a globally hypoelliptic, self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{G})
, with an associated complete system of smooth generalized eigenfunctions. Moreover, it holds:

\|(\xi_{1},...,\xi_{\kappa})\|_{p}^{\alpha}\lesssim\|\sigma_{\mathcal{L}_{sub}^{\alpha}}(\xi)\|_{inf},\quad\xi\in\Gamma,

where
\sigma_{\mathcal{L}_{sub}^{\alpha}}
denotes the symbol of the invariant operator
\mathcal{L}_{sub}^{\alpha}
.

1.4.
The concept of global hypoellipticity

When talking about the real case, global hypoellipticty comes in two versions, one local and one global, because we can talk about local and global differentiability. If
M
is a smooth manifold (over
\mathbb{R}
) and
L
is some differential operator, these definitions are:

•

Local hypoellipcity :
If
Lf
is differentiable
around

x_{0}\in M
, then
f
is differentiable around
x_{0}
.

•

Global hypoellipticity:
If
Lf
is differentiable in
the whole manifold

M
, then
f
is differentiable in the whole
M
.

Our interest here is the global hypoellipticity, which on compact smooth manifolds can actually be put very nicely in terms of the Fourier analysis associated to some elliptic operator. In particular for compact Lie groups the Fourier analysis is given in terms of the representation theory, where the matrix entries of the representations are
C^{\infty}
-eigenfunctions of the Laplace-Beltrami operator, considering the group as a compact smooth manifold. In that setting, the space of smooth functions can be completely described in terms of the Fourier transform:
f\in C^{\infty}(G)
if and only if
\|\widehat{f}(\xi)\|_{HS}\lesssim(1+\mu_{[\xi]})^{-k/2}
, for any
k\geq 0
and any
[\xi]\in\widehat{G}
, where
\mu_{[\xi]}
denotes the eigenvalue of the Laplace-Beltrami operator associated to
[\xi]\in\widehat{G}
. Here
\widehat{G}
denotes the unitary dual of
G
, and
\widehat{f}(\xi)
is defined as:

\widehat{f}(\xi):=\int_{G}f(x)\xi^{*}(x)dx,\quad\|f(\xi)\|_{HS}:=Tr[\widehat{f}(\xi)\widehat{f}(\xi)^{*}]^{1/2},

and
\|\cdot\|_{HS}
denotes the
Hilbert-Schmidt
norm of a linear operator. As a direct consequence, see for instance
[
18
]
, an invariant pseudo-differential operator

T_{\sigma}f(x):=\sum_{[\xi]\in\widehat{G}}d_{\xi}Tr[\xi(x)\sigma(\xi)\widehat{f}(\xi)],\quad f\in C^{\infty}(G),

defines a globally hypoelliptic operator if and only if, for all except finitely many
[\xi]\in\widehat{G}
and some
C>0
, we have

C(1+\mu_{[\xi]})^{s/2}\leq\|\sigma(\xi)\|_{inf}:=\inf\{\|\sigma(\xi)v\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\xi}}\,:\,\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\xi}}=1\},

where
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mu_{[\xi]}
denotes the eigenvalue of the Laplace-Beltrami operator associated to
[\xi]\in\widehat{G}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{\xi}
is the corresponding representation space.

In the case of metrizable profinite groups, and even more general ultrametric spaces, the definition of differentiability is replaced by the Vladimirov-Taibleson operator as it is discussed in
[
13
]
, which serves as an analogue to the Laplace-Beltrami operator from the real case. See for instance
[
2
]
. In this paper we are interested specifically on nilpotent Lie groups over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, which we will always think of as
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}
endowed with some non-commutative operation ”
\star
”. In this kind of groups the Vladimirov-Taibleson operator on
\mathbb{G}
has the particular form

D^{\alpha}f(\mathbf{x}):=\frac{1-p^{\alpha}}{1-p^{-(\alpha+d)}}\int_{\mathbb{G}}\frac{f(\mathbf{x}\star\mathbf{y}^{-1})-f(\mathbf{x})}{\|\mathbf{y}\|_{p}^{\alpha+d}}d\mathbf{y}.

Here
dy
denotes the unique normalized Haar measure on
\mathbb{G}
. The right analogue of a
C^{\infty}
-function is a
Schwartz function
, which is a square integrable function
f
with the property that

D^{\alpha}f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{G}),\quad\text{for all}\,\,\alpha>0.

Actually, on compact nilpotent
p
-adic Lie groups the right definition of Sobolev spaces is given in terms of the invariant operator operator

\mathbb{D}^{\alpha}f(\mathbf{x}):=\frac{1-p^{-d}}{1-p^{-(\alpha+d)}}f(\mathbf{x})+\frac{1-p^{\alpha}}{1-p^{-(\alpha+d)}}\int_{\mathbb{G}}\frac{f(\mathbf{x}\star\textbf{y}^{-1})-f(\mathbf{x})}{\|\textbf{y}\|_{p}^{\alpha+d}}d\mathbf{y},

where

W^{k,p}(\mathbb{G}):=\{f\in L^{r}(\mathbb{G})\,:\,\mathbb{D}^{\alpha}f\in L^{r}(\mathbb{G})\},\quad\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G}):=\bigcap_{k>0}W^{k,p}(\mathbb{G}),\quad 1\leq p<\infty.

It is not hard to see how the matrix coefficients of a compact nilpotent
p
-adic Lie group are actually eigenfunctions of
\mathbb{D}^{\alpha}
, and for a given
[\xi]\in\widehat{G}
, all the functions
\xi_{ij}
share the same eigenvalues. We will denote by
\langle\xi\rangle_{\mathbb{G}}
the eigenvalue of
\mathbb{D}^{\alpha}
corresponding to
[\xi]\in\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
. The problem of global hypoellipticity on a compact nilpotent
p
-adic Lie groups has two different versions, due to the existence of two different notions of smooth functions:

•

What we call
smooth functions
in this context, is the collection of locally constant functions on
\mathbb{G}
with a fixed index of local constancy, which we denote along the paper by
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G})
. That is

\quad\quad\quad\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G})=\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{G})\,:\,\text{There is an}\,\,n_{f}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\,\,\text{such that}\,\,f(\mathbf{x}\star\textbf{y})=f(\mathbf{x}),\quad\text{for}\,\,\|\textbf{y}\|_{p}\leq p^{-n_{f}}\}.

This is going to be the initial domain of all of our operators, and its finite dimensional sub-spaces are very important for our analysis. Via Fourier transform,
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G})
if and only if
\widehat{f}(\xi)=0
for all but finitely many
[\xi]\in\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
.

•

The second relevant space is the
Schwartz space
, which via Fourier transform can be written as

\quad\quad\quad\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})=\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{G})\,:\,\|\widehat{f}(\xi)\|_{HS}\leq C\langle\xi\rangle_{\mathbb{G}}^{-k},\,\text{for all but finitely many}\,\,[\xi]\in\widehat{G}\,\,\text{and all}\,\,k>0\}.

This is going to be the relevant space for our discussion, so that our definition of global hypoellipticity is the following:

Definition 1.8
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a compact nilpotent
p
-adic Lie group. We say that an invariant pseudo-differential operator
T_{\sigma}
is a
globally hypoelliptic operator
, if and only if the condition
T_{\sigma}f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
implies that
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
.

In a similar way as it is proven in
[
18
]
, an invariant operator with associated symbol
\sigma
is a globally hypoelliptic operator if and only if

C\langle\xi\rangle_{\mathbb{G}}^{\alpha}\leq\|\sigma(\xi)\|_{inf},\quad\text{for some}\,\,C>0,\,\,\alpha\in\mathbb{R},\,\,\text{and all but finitely many}\,[\xi]\in\widehat{\mathbb{G}}.

According to Theorem
1.7
, if
\mathbb{G}
has dimension
d\leq 5
then the elements of
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
are indexed by some subset of
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{d}\cong\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}
, and it also holds that

\langle\xi\rangle_{\mathbb{G}}^{\alpha}=\begin{cases}\frac{1-p^{-d}}{1-p^{-(\alpha+d)}},&\,\text{if}\,\|\xi\|_{p}=1,\\
\|\xi\|_{p}^{\alpha}I_{d_{\xi}}.\,&\,\text{in other case,}\end{cases}

for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. So, actually the condition for the global hypoellipticity in our setting is going to be

C\|\xi\|_{p}^{\alpha}\leq\|\widehat{f}(\xi)\|_{inf},\quad\text{for all but finitely many}\,\,[\xi]\in\widehat{\mathbb{G}},\,\,\text{and some}\,\alpha\in\mathbb{R}.

Our goal now is to present some interesting operators for which the previous condition holds. For instance, according to Theorem
1.3
, the Fourier series of a square integrable function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}_{d})
on the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}_{d}
has the form

f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},z)=\sum_{\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}\sum_{(\xi,\eta)\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{2d}/{p^{\vartheta(\lambda)}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}}|\lambda|_{p}^{d}Tr[\pi_{(\xi,\eta,\lambda)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},z)\widehat{f}(\xi,\eta,\lambda)],

so that an invariant operator looks like

T_{\sigma}f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},z)=\sum_{\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}\sum_{(\xi,\eta)\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{2d}/p^{\vartheta(\lambda)}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}}|\lambda|_{p}^{d}Tr[\pi_{(\xi,\eta,\lambda)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},z)\sigma(\xi,\eta,\lambda)\widehat{f}(\xi,\eta,\lambda)],

where
\sigma
is a mapping

\sigma:\widehat{\mathbb{H}}_{d}\to\bigcup_{(\xi,\eta,\lambda)\in\widehat{\mathbb{H}}_{d}}\mathbb{C}^{|\lambda|_{p}^{d}\times|\lambda|_{p}^{d}},\quad\sigma(\xi,\eta,\lambda)\in\mathbb{C}^{|\lambda|_{p}^{d}\times|\lambda|_{p}^{d}}.

In this way, observing how the Vladimirov sub-Laplacian is an invariant operator, we can prove the following spectral theorem on
\mathbb{H}_{d}
:

Theorem 1.9
.

Let
p>2
. Let
\mathfrak{h}_{d}
be the
(2d+1)
-dimensional Heisenberg Lie algebra, with generators

\{X_{1},...,X_{d},Y_{1},...,Y_{d},Z\},\,\,\,[X_{i},Y_{j}]=\delta_{ij}Z,

and let
\textbf{V}=\{V_{1},...,V_{d}\}\subset\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\{X_{1},...,X_{d}\},\,\,\textbf{W}=\{W_{1},...,W_{d}\}\subset\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\{Y_{1},...,Y_{d}\}
be collections of linearly independent vectors. The Vladimirov sub-Laplacian associated to this collection

T_{\textbf{V},\textbf{W}}^{\alpha}:=\sum_{k=1}^{\varkappa}\partial_{V_{k}}^{\alpha}+\partial_{W_{k}}^{\alpha},

defines a left-invariant, self-adjoint, globally hypoelliptic operator on
\mathbb{H}_{d}
. The spectrum of this operator is purely punctual, and its associated eigenfunctions form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}_{d})
. Furthermore, the symbol of
T_{\textbf{V},\textbf{W}}^{\alpha}
acts on each representation space as a
p
-adic Schrödinger operator, and the space
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}_{d})
can be written as the direct sum

L^{2}(\mathbb{H}_{d})=\overline{\bigoplus_{(\xi,\eta,\lambda)\in\widehat{\mathbb{H}}_{d}}\bigoplus_{h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}/p^{-\vartheta(\lambda)}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}}\mathcal{V}_{(\xi,\eta,\lambda)}^{h^{\prime}}},\,\,\,\mathcal{V}_{(\xi,\eta,\lambda)}=\bigoplus_{h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}/p^{-\vartheta(\lambda)}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}}\mathcal{V}_{(\xi,\eta,\lambda)}^{h^{\prime}},

where each finite-dimensional sub-space

\mathcal{V}_{(\xi,\eta,\lambda)}^{h^{\prime}}:=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{(\pi_{(\xi,\eta,\lambda)})_{hh^{\prime}}\,:\,h\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}/p^{-\vartheta(\lambda)}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}\},

is an invariant sub-space where
T_{\textbf{V},\textbf{W}}^{\alpha}
acts like the Schrödinger-type operator operator

\sum_{k=1}^{d}\partial_{V_{k}}^{\alpha}+|W_{k}\cdot(\lambda h^{\prime}+\eta)|_{p}^{\alpha}-\frac{1-p^{-1}}{1-p^{-(\alpha+1)}}.

Consequently, the spectrum of
T_{\textbf{V},\textbf{W}}^{\alpha}
restricted to
\mathcal{V}_{(\xi,\eta,\lambda)}^{h^{\prime}}
is given by

Spec(T_{\textbf{V},\textbf{W}}^{\alpha}|_{\mathcal{V}_{(\xi,\eta,\lambda)}^{h}})=\Big\{\sum_{k=1}^{d}|V_{k}\cdot(\tau+\xi)|_{p}^{\alpha}+|W_{k}\cdot(\lambda h^{\prime}+\eta)|_{p}^{\alpha}-2\frac{1-p^{-1}}{1-p^{-(\alpha+1)}}\,:\,1\leq\|\tau\|_{p}\leq|\lambda|_{p}\Big\},

so that
Spec(T_{\textbf{V},\textbf{W}}^{\alpha})
is going to be the collection of real numbers

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{d}|V_{k}\cdot(\tau+\xi)|_{p}^{\alpha}+|W_{k}\cdot(\lambda h^{\prime}+\eta)|_{p}^{\alpha}-2\frac{1-p^{-1}}{1-p^{-(\alpha+1)}},

where
(\xi,\eta,\lambda)\in\widehat{\mathbb{H}}_{d},\,h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}/p^{-\vartheta(\lambda)}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d},\,1\leq\|\tau\|_{p}\leq|\lambda|_{p}
, and the corresponding eigenfunctions are given by

\mathscr{e}_{(\xi,\eta,\lambda),h^{\prime},\tau}(\mathbf{x})=e^{2\pi i\{(\xi+\tau)\cdot\mathbf{x}+\eta\cdot\mathbf{y}+\lambda(z+h^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{y})\}_{p}},\,\,\,\,(\xi,\eta,\lambda)\in\widehat{\mathbb{H}}_{d},\,h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}/p^{-\vartheta(\lambda)}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d},\,1\leq\|\tau\|_{p}\leq|\lambda|_{p}.

Remark 1.10
.

The restriction of the Vladimirov sub-Laplacian to each representation space acts in a similar way as a the
p
-adic Schrodinger operators studied in
[
30
]
. However, in that reference the authors take a rather different approach, calculating the eigenspaces associated to a certain eigenvalue and estimating the associated multiplicities. Here we found a very convenient form to decompose the operator
D^{\alpha}+V
, but this is not very useful to find out some desirable information like the multiplicity of each eigenvalue. Unfortunately, investigating this kind of question is beyond the scope of this paper.

1.5.
Organization of the paper

In order to prove our results, we will organize our ideas in the following way:

•

In Section 2 we recall the basic facts about
p
-adic analysis we will be using. A key detail in this section will be the introduction of
p
-adic Gaussian integrals studied in
[
30
]
, which are essential for the analysis of certain characters of representations. Apart from that, we will recall in this section the basic elemets of the analysis on
\mathbb{H}_{d}
and
\mathcal{B}_{4}
. With these two groups we have covered already dimensions
3
and
4
, and one of the groups of dimension
5
: the
5
-dimensional Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}_{2}=\mathbf{exp}(\mathfrak{g}^{5,1})
. This serve us as a preparation to continue proving Theorem
1.7
on the compact nilpotent
p
-adic Lie groups with dimension
d=5
,
\mathbb{G}^{5,i}=\mathbf{exp}(\mathfrak{g}^{5,i})
,
2\leq i\leq 6
. Once again, we emphasize the importance of stating Theorems
1.3
and
2.6
, as the descriptions of the unitary duals of
\mathbb{H}_{d}
and
\mathcal{B}_{4}
will be essential for analyzing the duals of some of our
5
-dimensional groups, due to the fact that certain representations of
5
-dimensional groups descend to representations of lower-dimensional groups. For detailed proofs and the complete calculations on
\mathbb{H}_{d}
and
\mathcal{B}_{4}
, we refer the reader to
[
29
,
28
]
.

•

In Sections 3 to 7 we explain the proof of Theorem
1.7
in the particular cases of
\mathbb{G}^{5,i}
,
1\leq i\leq 6
. The arguments for each group follow the same lines as for
\mathbb{H}_{d}
, but sometimes it is necessary to introduce some extra details, like
p
-adic Gaussian integrals or Hensel’s lemma. hopefully, this will provide some light about the more general case of nilpotent
p
-adic Lie groups over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

•

In Section 8 we discuss briefly our results, and some future challenges.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
p
-adic numbers and Gaussian integrals

Throughout this section
p>2
will denote a fixed prime number. The field of
p
-adic numbers, usually denoted by
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, can be defined as the completion of the field of rational numbers
\mathbb{Q}
with respect to the
p
-adic norm
|\cdot|_{p}
, defined as

|u|_{p}:=\begin{cases}0&\text{ }\text{if}\text{ }u=0,\\
p^{-l}&\text{ }\text{if}\text{ }u=p^{l}\frac{a}{b},\end{cases}

where
a
and
b
are integers coprime with
p
. The integer
l:=\vartheta(u)
, with
\vartheta(0):=+\infty
, is called the
p
-adic valuation
of
u
. The unit ball of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{d}
with the
p
-adic norm

\|u\|_{p}:=\max_{1\leq j\leq d}|u_{j}|_{p},

is called the group of
p
-adic integers, and it will be denoted by
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}
. Any
p
-adic number
u\neq 0
has a unique expansion of the form

u=p^{\vartheta(u)}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}u_{j}p^{j},

where
u_{j}\in\{0,1,...,p-1\}
and
u_{0}\neq 0
. With this expansion we define the fractional part of
u\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, denoted by
\{u\}_{p}
, as the rational number

\{u\}_{p}:=\begin{cases}0&\text{ }\text{if}\text{ }u=0\text{ }\text{or}\text{ }\vartheta(u)\geq 0,\\
p^{\vartheta(u)}\sum_{j=0}^{-\vartheta(u)-1}u_{j}p^{j},&\text{ }\text{if}\text{ }ord(u)<0.\end{cases}

Definition 2.1
.

Given a vector
u\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{d}
, we define its associated
p
-adic valuation
as the minimum among the valuations
\vartheta(u_{j})
,
1\leq j\leq d
, that is

\vartheta(u):=\min_{1\leq j\leq d}\vartheta(u_{j}).

The ring
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}
is compact, totally disconected, i.e. profinite, and abelian. Its dual group in the sense of Pontryagin, the collection of characters of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}
, will be denoted by
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{d}
. The dual group of the
p
-adic integers is known to be the Prüfer group
\mathbb{Z}(p^{\infty})
, the unique
p
-group in which every element has
p
different
p
-th roots. The Prüfer group may be identified with the quotient group
\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. In this way, each character of the group
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}
may be written as

\chi_{p}(\tau u):=e^{2\pi i\{\tau\cdot u\}_{p}},\text{ }\text{ }u\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d},\text{ }\tau\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{d}\cong\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d},

where
\tau
is some fixed element of
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{d}.

By the Peter–Weyl theorem, the elements of
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{d}
constitute an orthonormal basis for the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d})
, which provide us a Fourier analysis for suitable functions defined on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
in such a way that the formula

\varphi(u)=\sum_{\tau\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{d}}\widehat{\varphi}(\tau)\chi_{p}(\tau u),

holds almost everywhere in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Here
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}}[\varphi]
denotes the Fourier transform of
f
, in turn defined as

\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}}[\varphi](\tau):=\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}}\varphi(u)\overline{\chi_{p}(\tau u)}du,

where
du
is the normalised Haar measure on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}
.

Another important tool from
p
-adic analysis that we are going to be using, is the calculation of the
p
-adic Gaussian integral on the disk, see
[
30
, p.p. 65]
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
p\neq 2
be a prime number, and take any
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then, for any
a,b\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, we have

\int_{p^{\gamma}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}e^{2\pi i\{au^{2}+bu\}_{p}}du=\begin{cases}p^{-\gamma}\mathds{1}_{p^{-\gamma}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(b),&\,\,\text{if}\,\,\,|a|_{p}\leq p^{2\gamma},\\
\lambda_{p}(a)|a|_{p}^{-1/2}e^{2\pi i\{\frac{-b^{2}}{4a}\}_{p}}\mathds{1}_{p^{\gamma}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(b/a)&\,\,\,\text{if}\,\,\,|a|_{p}>p^{2\gamma}.\end{cases}

Here
\lambda_{p}:\mathbb{Q}_{p}\to\mathbb{C}
is the function defined as

\lambda_{p}(a):=\begin{cases}1,&\,\,\text{if}\,\,ord(u)\,\text{is even,}\\
\big(\frac{a_{0}}{p}\big),&\,\,\text{if}\,\,ord(u)\,\text{is odd and}\,\,p\equiv 1(\mathrm{mod}\,4),\\
i\big(\frac{a_{0}}{p}\big),&\,\,\text{if}\,\,ord(u)\,\text{is odd and}\,\,p\equiv 3(\mathrm{mod}\,4),\end{cases}

where
(\frac{a_{0}}{p})
denotes the Legendre symbol of
a_{0}
. Here, to simplify our notation, we are going to define

\Lambda(a,b):=\lambda_{p}(a)|a|_{p}^{-1/2}e^{2\pi i\{\frac{-b^{2}}{4a}\}_{p}},

so that
|\Lambda(a,b)|=|a|_{p}^{-1/2}
, and in this way

\int_{p^{\gamma}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}e^{2\pi i\{au^{2}+bu\}_{p}}du=p^{-\gamma}\mathds{1}_{p^{-2\gamma}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(a)\cdot\mathds{1}_{p^{-\gamma}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(b)+\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}\setminus p^{-2\gamma}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(a)\cdot\Lambda(a,b)\cdot\mathds{1}_{p^{\gamma}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(b/a).

With this tool we can prove the following auxiliary lemma, which is simply the calculation of the
L^{2}
-norm of a function given in terms of
p
-adic Gaussian integral:

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
(x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2}
, and take
\xi_{3},\xi_{4}\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
. Then

\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2}}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}e^{2\pi i\{(\xi_{3}x_{2}+\xi_{4}x_{3})u+\xi_{4}x_{2}\frac{u^{2}}{2}\}_{p}}du\Big|^{2}dx_{2}dx_{3}=\max\{|\xi_{3}|_{p},|\xi_{4}|_{p}\}^{-1}=\|(\xi_{3},\xi_{4})\|_{p}^{-1}.

Proof.

Let us define the auxiliary function

f(x_{2},x_{3}):=\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}e^{2\pi i\{(\xi_{3}x_{2}+\xi_{4}x_{3})u+\xi_{4}x_{2}\frac{u^{2}}{2}\}_{p}}du.

Then, as a function on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2}
, we can compute its Fourier transform as

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2}}[f](\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}e^{2\pi i\{(\xi_{3}x_{2}+\xi_{4}x_{3})u+\xi_{4}x_{2}\frac{u^{2}}{2}-\alpha x_{2}-\beta x_{3}\}_{p}}dudx_{2}dx_{3}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2}}e^{2\pi i\{(\xi_{3}u+\xi_{4}\frac{u^{2}}{2}-\alpha)x_{2}-(\xi_{4}u-\beta)x_{3}\}_{p}}dx_{2}dx_{3}du

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\xi_{3}u+\xi_{4}\frac{u^{2}}{2}-\alpha)\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\xi_{4}u-\beta)du.

Notice how
|\xi_{4}u-\beta|_{p}\leq 1
implies that
\xi_{4}u=\beta
in
\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. In this way, we have the following equality modulo
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
:

\xi_{3}u+\xi_{4}\frac{u^{2}}{2}=\frac{u}{2}\big(2\xi_{3}+\xi_{4}u\big)=\frac{u}{2}\big(2\xi_{3}+\beta\big)\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p},

therefore

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2}}[f](\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(u(\xi_{3}+\beta/2)-\alpha)\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\xi_{4}u-\beta)du

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathds{1}_{\frac{\alpha}{\xi_{3}+\beta/2}+p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{3}+\beta/2)}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(u)\mathds{1}_{\frac{\beta}{\xi_{4}}+p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{4})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(u)du

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\Big(\big[\frac{\alpha}{\xi_{3}+\beta/2}+p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{3}+\beta/2)}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\big]\cap\big[\frac{\beta}{\xi_{4}}+p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{4})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\big]\Big),

where
\mu_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}}
denotes the normalized Haar measure of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}
. To continue we need to see how, since we are integrating over
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, in order for the above integral to be different from zero we need

\big|\frac{\alpha}{\xi_{3}+\beta/2}\big|_{p}\leq 1,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\big|\frac{\beta}{\xi_{4}}\big|_{p}\leq 1,

so that only finitely many terms
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2}}[f](\alpha,\beta)
can be non-zero. Also

\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2}}[f](\alpha,\beta)=\begin{cases}0,&\,\,\text{if the balls are disjoint},\\
\max\{|\xi_{4}|_{p},|\xi_{3}+\beta/2|_{p}\}^{-1},&\,\,\text{if the balls are not disjoint}.\end{cases}

Finally, we use this Fourier transform to calculate the
L^{2}
-norm of
f
in two different cases. When
|\xi_{3}|_{p}>|\xi_{4}|_{p}
, we have
|\xi_{3}+\beta/2|_{p}=|\xi_{3}|_{p}
, so that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{2}}\mu_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\Big(\big[\frac{\alpha}{\xi_{3}+\beta/2}+p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{3}+\beta/2)}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\big]\cap\big[\frac{\beta}{\xi_{4}}+p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{4})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\big]\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{2}}\mu_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\Big(\big[\frac{\alpha}{\xi_{3}+\beta/2}+p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{3})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\big]\cap\big[\frac{\beta}{\xi_{4}}+p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{4})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\big]\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}|\xi_{3}|_{p}^{-1}\sum_{\alpha\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}\mu_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\Big(\big[\frac{\alpha}{\xi_{3}+\beta/2}+p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{3})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\big]\cap\big[\frac{\beta}{\xi_{4}}+p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{4})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\big]\Big)

\displaystyle=|\xi_{3}|_{p}^{-1}\sum_{\beta\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}\mu_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\Big(\frac{\beta}{\xi_{4}}+p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{4})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\Big)=|\xi_{3}|_{p}^{-1}.

When
|\xi_{3}|_{p}\leq|\xi_{4}|_{p},
we get
|\xi_{3}+\beta/2|_{p}\leq|\xi_{4}|_{p}
, so we conclude in a similar way

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{2}}\mu_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\Big(\big[\frac{\alpha}{\xi_{3}+\beta/2}+p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{3}+\beta/2)}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\big]\cap\big[\frac{\beta}{\xi_{4}}+p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{4})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\big]\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}\sum_{\beta\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}|\xi_{4}|_{p}^{-1}\mu_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\Big(\big[\frac{\alpha}{\xi_{3}+\beta/2}+p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{3}+\beta/2)}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\big]\cap\big[\frac{\beta}{\xi_{4}}+p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{4})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\big]\Big)

\displaystyle=|\xi_{4}|^{-1}_{p}\sum_{\alpha\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}\mu_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\Big(\frac{\alpha}{\xi_{3}+\beta/2}+p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{3}+\beta/2)}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\Big)=|\xi_{4}|_{p}^{-1}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

A more general version of this lemma, which we need to handle a more complicated Gaussian integral, is the following:

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\xi\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{3}
and
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2}
. Consider the function

f(x_{1},x_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}e^{2\pi i\{\xi_{1}x_{1}u^{2}+(\xi_{2}x_{1}+\xi_{3}x_{2})\}_{p}}du.

Then
\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2})}^{2}=\|\xi\|_{p}^{-1}.

Proof.

We only need to see how

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2}}[f](\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\xi_{1}u^{2}+\xi_{2}-\gamma_{1}u)\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\xi_{3}u-\gamma_{2})du

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\{u:\,\xi_{1}u^{2}+\xi_{2}u-\gamma_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\}\cap\{u:\,\xi_{3}u-\gamma_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\}),

where
\mu_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
denotes the unique normalized Haar measure on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Now we consider cases:

•

We know that
\xi_{1}u(u+\xi_{2}/\xi_{1})-\gamma_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is possible only for
|\gamma_{1}|_{p}\leq\|(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\|_{p}
. Also, it should be clear that

\sum_{|\gamma_{1}|_{p}\leq\|(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\|_{p}}\mu_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\{u:\,\xi_{1}u^{2}+\xi_{2}u-\gamma_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\})=1.

Since it holds

h_{1}(u)=\xi_{1}u^{2}+\xi_{2}u,\quad h_{1}(u+v)=h_{1}(u),\quad\text{for}\,\,|v|_{p}\leq\|(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\|_{p}^{-1},

each set
\{u:\,\xi_{1}u^{2}+\xi_{2}u-\gamma_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\}
should me expressible as a characteristic function of a
p
-adic ball of radius
\|(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\|_{p}^{-1}
.

•

Similarly,
\xi_{3}u-\gamma_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is possible only for
|\gamma_{2}|_{p}\leq|\xi_{3}|_{p}
. Also,

\sum_{|\gamma_{2}|_{p}\leq|\xi_{3}|_{p}}\mu_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\{u:\,\xi_{3}u^{2}-\gamma_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\})=1.

Since it holds

h_{2}(u)=\xi_{3}u,\quad h_{2}(u+v)=h_{2}(u),\quad\text{for}\,\,|v|_{p}\leq|\xi_{3}|_{p}^{-1},

each set
\{u:\,\xi_{3}u-\gamma_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\}
should be expressible as a characteristic function of a
p
-adic ball of radius
|\xi_{3}|_{p}^{-1}
.

In conclusion, each set

\{u:\,\xi_{1}u^{2}+\xi_{2}u-\gamma_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\}\cap\{u:\,\xi_{3}u-\gamma_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\}

is the intersection of two balls, at least one of them with measure
\|\xi\|_{p}^{-1}
, so that we can write

\widehat{f}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})=\mathbf{N}_{\xi}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\|\xi\|_{p}^{-1},

where
\mathbf{N}_{\xi}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
is always zero or one, depending on whether the intersections of the balls are empty or not. In conclusion:

\displaystyle\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{2}}|\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2}}[f](\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{2}}\mathbf{N}_{\xi}^{2}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\|\xi\|_{p}^{-2}

\displaystyle=\|\xi\|_{p}^{-1}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{2}}\mathbf{N}_{\xi}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\|\xi\|_{p}^{-1}=\|\xi\|_{p}^{-1},

where we used the fact that
\mathbf{N}_{\xi}^{2}=\mathbf{N}_{\xi}
. The proof is concluded.
∎

2.2.
The Heisenberg group over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}

Let
p>2
be a prime number. Let us denote by
\mathbb{H}_{d}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
the
(2d+1)
-dimensional Heisenberg group over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, or simply
\mathbb{H}_{d}
for short, here defined as

\mathbb{H}_{d}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})=\left\{\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{x}^{t}&z\\
0&I_{d}&\mathbf{y}\\
0&0&1\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{GL}_{d+2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\,:\,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d},\,z\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\right\}.

Clearly
\mathbb{H}_{d}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is a compact analytic
d
-dimensional manifold, which is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2d+1}
. Moreover, the operations on
\mathbb{H}_{d}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
are analytic functions, making
\mathbb{H}_{d}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
a
p
-adic Lie group. Let us denote by
\mathfrak{h}_{d}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
its associated
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-Lie algebra. We can write explicitly

\mathfrak{h}_{d}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})=\left\{\begin{bmatrix}0&\textbf{a}^{t}&c-\frac{\textbf{a}\cdot\textbf{b}}{2}\\
0&0_{d}&\textbf{b}\\
0&0&0\end{bmatrix}\in\mathcal{M}_{d+2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\,:\,\textbf{a},\textbf{b}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d},c\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\right\}.

Recall how for an element of the Lie algebra

u:=\begin{bmatrix}0&\textbf{a}^{t}&c-\frac{\textbf{a}\cdot\textbf{b}}{2}\\
0&0_{d}&\textbf{b}\\
0&0&0\end{bmatrix},

the exponential map evaluates to

\textbf{exp}(u)=\begin{bmatrix}1&\textbf{a}^{t}&c\\
0&I_{d}&\textbf{b}\\
0&0&1\end{bmatrix}.

The exponential map transform sub-ideals of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{d}
to subgroups of
\mathbb{H}_{d}
. Actually, we can turn the exponential map into a group homomorphism by using the Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff formula. Let us define the operation “
\star
” on
\mathfrak{h}_{d}
by

X\star Y:=X+Y+\frac{1}{2}[X,Y].

Then clearly
(\mathfrak{h}_{d},\star)\cong\mathbb{H}_{d}
is a profinite topological group, and it can be endowed with the sequence of subgroups
J_{n}:=(\mathfrak{h}_{d}(p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}),\star)
, where

\mathfrak{h}_{d}(p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p})=p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}X_{1}+...+p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}X_{d}+p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}Y_{1}+...+p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}Y_{d}+p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}Z,

so
\mathbb{H}_{d}
is a compact Vilenkin group, together with the sequence of compact open subgroups

G_{n}:=\mathbb{H}_{d}(p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p})=\textbf{exp}(\mathfrak{h}_{d}(p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p})),\,\,\,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Notice how the sequence
\mathscr{G}=\{G_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
forms a basis of neighbourhoods at the identity, so the group is metrizable, and we can endow it with the natural ultrametric

|(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},z)\star(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime},z^{\prime})^{-1}|_{\mathscr{G}}:=\begin{cases}0&\,\,\text{if}\,(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},z)=(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime},z^{\prime}),\\
|G_{n}|=p^{-n(2d+1)}&\,\,\text{if}\,(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},z)\star(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime},z^{\prime})^{-1}\in G_{n}\setminus G_{n+1}.\end{cases}

Nevertheless, instead of this ultrametric we will use the
p
-adic norm

\|(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},z)\|_{p}:=\max\{\|\mathbf{x}\|_{p},\|\mathbf{y}\|_{p},|z|_{p}\}.

Remark 2.5
.

Notice that how
\|(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},z)\|_{p}^{2d+1}=|(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},z)|_{\mathscr{G}},
for any
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},z)\in\mathbb{H}_{d},
and we also have that

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},z)\star(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime},z^{\prime})^{-1}\|_{p}=\|(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x^{\prime}},\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y^{\prime}},z-z^{\prime}+\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{y^{\prime}})\|_{p}.

From the above it is clear how

\|(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},z)\star(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime},z^{\prime})^{-1}\|_{p}=0\iff(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},z)=(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime},z^{\prime}),

proving that
\|\cdot\|_{p}
is a valid choice for an alternative distance function on
\mathbb{H}_{d}
. The reason to use the
p
-adic norm instead of the Vilenkin distance function is to make the Vladimirov-Taibleson operator to look the same as for the abelian case
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}
.

2.3.
The Engel group over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}

Let
p>2
be a prime number. Let us denote by
\mathfrak{b}_{4}
the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-Lie algebra generated by
X_{1},..,X_{4}
, with the commutation relations

[X_{1},X_{2}]=X_{3},\,\,\,[X_{1},X_{3}]=X_{4},\,\,\,\mathfrak{b}_{4}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{3}\mathcal{V}_{j},

where

\mathcal{V}_{1}=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\{X_{1},X_{2}\},\,\,\,\mathcal{V}_{1}=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\{X_{3}\},\,\,\,\mathcal{V}_{3}=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\{X_{4}\}.

We call the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-Lie algebra
\mathfrak{b}_{4}
the
4
-dimensional
Engel algebra
, and its exponential image, which we denote here by
\mathcal{B}_{4}
, is called the
Engel group
over the
p
-adic integers.
Let us consider the realization of
\mathfrak{b}_{4}
as the matrix algebra

\mathfrak{b}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})=\left\{\begin{bmatrix}0&x_{1}&0&x_{4}-\frac{1}{2}x_{1}(x_{3}-\frac{1}{2}x_{1}x_{2})-\frac{1}{6}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}\\
0&0&x_{1}&x_{3}-\frac{1}{2}x_{1}x_{2}\\
0&0&0&x_{2}\\
0&0&0&0\\
\end{bmatrix}\in\mathcal{M}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\,:\,x\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{4}\right\}.

With this realization, and using the usual matrix exponential map, we can think on
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
as the matrix group

\mathcal{B}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})=\left\{\begin{bmatrix}1&x_{1}&\frac{1}{2}x_{1}^{2}&x_{4}\\
0&1&x_{1}&x_{3}\\
0&0&1&x_{2}\\
0&0&0&1\\
\end{bmatrix}\in\mathcal{M}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\,:\,x\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{4}\right\},

which is analytically isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{4}
with the operation

\mathbf{x}\star\mathbf{y}:=(x_{1}+y_{1},x_{2}+y_{2},x_{3}+y_{3}+x_{1}y_{2},x_{4}+y_{4}+x_{1}y_{3}+\frac{1}{2}x_{1}^{2}y_{2}).

The exponential map transforms sub-ideals of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{b}_{4}
to subgroups of
\mathcal{B}_{4}\cong(\mathfrak{b}_{4},*)
, which can be endowed with the sequence of subgroups
J_{n}:=(\mathfrak{b}_{4}(p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}),*)
, where

\mathfrak{b}_{4}(p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p})=p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}X_{1}+p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}X_{2}+p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}X_{3}+p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}X_{4},

so
\mathcal{B}_{4}
is a compact Vilenkin group, together with the sequence of compact open subgroups

G_{n}:=\mathcal{B}_{4}(p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p})=\textbf{exp}(\mathfrak{b}_{4}(p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p})),\,\,\,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Notice how the sequence
\mathscr{G}=\{G_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
forms a basis of neighbourhoods at the identity, so the group is metrizable, and we can endow it with the natural ultrametric

|\mathbf{x}\star\mathbf{y}^{-1}|_{\mathscr{G}}:=\begin{cases}0&\,\,\text{if}\,\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y},\\
|G_{n}|=p^{-4n}&\,\,\text{if}\,\mathbf{x}\star\mathbf{y}^{-1}\in G_{n}\setminus G_{n+1}.\end{cases}

Nevertheless, instead of this ultrametric we will use the
p
-adic norm

\|\mathbf{x}\|_{p}:=\max_{1\leq j\leq 4}\|x_{j}\|_{p},.

Notice that how
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{p}^{4}=|\mathbf{x}|_{\mathscr{G}},
for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{B}_{4}

The group
\mathcal{B}_{4}
is another important example where a version of Theorem
1.7
holds. In dimension
5
, given that nilpotent groups of nilpotency index
\mathscr{N}
are extensions of nilpotent groups with lower indices and dimensions, the representation theory of
5
-dimensional groups often reduces to that of dimensions
3
and
4
. Dimension
3
corresponds to the
compact Heisenberg group

\mathbb{H}_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, while dimension
4
is represented by
\mathcal{B}_{4}
, so that we need to include the representation theory of
\mathcal{B}_{4}
too:

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, or simply
\mathcal{B}_{4}
for short, be the
4
-dimensional Engel group over the
p
-adic integers. Let us denote by
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{4}
the unitary dual of
\mathcal{B}_{4}
, i.e., the collection of all unitary irreducible representations of
\mathcal{B}_{4}
. Then we can identify
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{4}
with the following subset of
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{4}\cong\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{4}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{4}
:

\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{4}:=\{\xi\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{4}\,:\,1\leq|\xi_{4}|_{p}<|\xi_{3}|_{p}\,\wedge\,(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\in\widehat{\mathbb{H}}_{1},\,\text{or}\,|\xi_{3}|_{p}=1\,\wedge\,\xi_{1}\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}/p^{\vartheta(\xi_{4})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\}.

Moreover, each non-trivial representation
[\pi_{\xi}]
is equivalent to one of the representation
[\pi_{\xi}]\in\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{4}
which can be realized in the following finite dimensional sub-space
\mathcal{H}_{\xi}
of
L^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
:

\mathcal{H}_{\xi}:=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\|(\xi_{3},\xi_{4})\|_{p}^{1/2}\mathds{1}_{h+p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{3},\xi_{4})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\,\,:\,h\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}/p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{3},\xi_{4})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\},

where
d_{\xi}:=dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{H}_{\xi})=\max\{|\xi_{3}|_{p},|\xi_{4}|_{p}\}
, and the representation acts on functions
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}_{\xi}
according to the formula

\pi_{\xi}(\mathbf{x})\varphi(u):=e^{2\pi i\{\xi_{1}x_{1}+\xi_{2}x_{2}+\xi_{3}(x_{3}+ux_{2})+\xi_{4}(x_{4}+ux_{3}+\frac{u^{2}}{2}x_{2})\}_{p}}\varphi(u+x_{1}).

With this explicit realization of
[\pi_{\xi}]\in\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{4}
, and with the natural choice of basis for each representation space, the associated matrix coefficients
(\pi_{\xi})_{hh^{\prime}}
are given by

(\pi_{\xi}(\mathbf{x}))_{hh^{\prime}}=e^{2\pi i\{\xi\cdot\mathbf{x}+(\xi_{3}x_{2}+\xi_{4}x_{3})(h^{\prime})+\xi_{4}x_{2}\frac{(h^{\prime})^{2}}{2}\}_{p}}\mathds{1}_{h^{\prime}-h+p^{\vartheta(\xi_{3},\xi_{4})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(x_{1}).

Sometimes we will use the notation

\mathcal{V}_{\xi}:=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{(\pi_{\xi})_{hh^{\prime}}\,:\,h,h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}/p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{3},\xi_{4})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\}.

See again Definition
2.1
for the definition of the
p
-adic valuation
\vartheta
.

Theorem
2.6
provides us with the following Fourier series representation of a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{B}_{4})
:

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\,\|(\xi_{3},\xi_{4})\|_{p}=1}\widehat{f}(\xi)e^{2\pi i\{\xi\cdot\mathbf{x}\}_{p}}+\sum_{\xi_{4}\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}\sum_{|\xi_{4}|_{p}<|\xi_{3}|_{p}}\sum_{(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{2}/p^{\vartheta(\xi_{3})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2}}|\xi_{3}|_{p}Tr[\pi_{\xi}(\mathbf{x})\widehat{f}(\xi)]

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\sum_{\,|\xi_{4}|_{p}>|\xi_{3}|_{p}=1}\sum_{\xi_{2}\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}\sum_{\xi_{1}\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}/p^{\vartheta(\xi_{4})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}|\xi_{4}|_{p}Tr[\pi_{\xi}(\mathbf{x})\widehat{f}(\xi)],

and in a similar way as for the Heisenberg group, we want to use this series representation to study linear invariant operators. For instance, with the description of the unitary dual given in Theorem
2.6
, we can prove the following spectral theorem for the Vladimirov sub-Laplacian on
\mathcal{B}_{4}
:

Theorem 2.7
.

Let
p>3
. The Vladimirov sub-Laplacian associated to the basis
\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
of the first stratum of
\mathfrak{b}_{4}

\mathscr{L}_{sub}^{\alpha}:=\partial_{X_{1}}^{\alpha}+\partial_{X_{2}}^{\alpha},

defines a left-invariant, self-adjoint, sub-elliptic operator on
\mathcal{B}_{4}
. The spectrum of this operator is purely punctual, and its associated eigenfunctions form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathcal{B}_{4})
. Furthermore, the symbol of
\mathscr{L}_{sub}^{\alpha}
acts on each representation space as a
p
-adic Schrödinger type operator, and the space
L^{2}(\mathcal{B}_{4})
can be written as the direct sum

L^{2}(\mathcal{B}_{4})=\overline{\bigoplus_{[\xi]\in\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{4}}\bigoplus_{h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}/p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{3},\xi_{4})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{V}_{\xi}^{h^{\prime}}},\,\,\,\mathcal{V}_{\xi}=\bigoplus_{{h^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}/p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{3},\xi_{4})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{V}_{\xi}^{h^{\prime}},

where each finite-dimensional sub-space

\mathcal{V}_{\xi}^{h^{\prime}}:=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{(\pi_{\xi})_{hh^{\prime}}\,:\,h\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}/p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{3},\xi_{4})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\},

is an invariant sub-space where
\mathscr{L}_{sub}^{\alpha}
acts like the Schrödinger-type operator operator

\partial_{x_{1}}^{\alpha}+Q(\xi,h^{\prime})-\frac{1-p^{-1}}{1-p^{-(\alpha+1)}}.

Consequently, the spectrum of
\mathscr{L}_{sub}^{\alpha}
restricted to
\mathcal{V}_{\xi}^{h^{\prime}}
is purely punctual, and it is given by

\begin{cases}|\xi_{1}|_{p}^{\alpha}+|\xi_{2}|^{\alpha}_{p}-2\frac{1-p^{-1}}{1-p^{-(\alpha+1)}}\quad&\text{if}\quad|\xi_{3}|_{p}=|\xi_{4}|_{p}=1,\\
|\xi_{1}+\tau|_{p}^{\alpha}+|\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}h^{\prime}|_{p}^{\alpha}-2\frac{1-p^{-1}}{1-p^{-(\alpha+1)}}\quad&\text{if}\quad 1=|\xi_{4}|_{p}<|\xi_{3}|_{p},\\
|\xi_{1}+\tau|_{p}^{\alpha}+|\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}h^{\prime}+\xi_{4}\frac{(h^{\prime})^{2}}{2}|_{p}^{\alpha}-2\frac{1-p^{-1}}{1-p^{-(\alpha+1)}}\quad&\text{if}\quad 1<|\xi_{4}|_{p}<|\xi_{3}|_{p},\\
|\xi_{1}+\tau|_{p}^{\alpha}+|\xi_{2}+\xi_{4}\frac{(h^{\prime})^{2}}{2}|_{p}^{\alpha}-2\frac{1-p^{-1}}{1-p^{-(\alpha+1)}}\quad&\text{if}\quad 1=|\xi_{3}|_{p}<|\xi_{4}|_{p},\end{cases}

where
\tau\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}/p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{3},\xi_{4})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, and the corresponding eigenfunctions are given by

\mathscr{e}_{\xi,h^{\prime},\tau}(\mathbf{x}):=e^{2\pi i\{\xi x+\tau x_{1}+(\xi_{3}x_{2}+\xi_{4}x_{3})(h^{\prime})+\xi_{4}x_{2}\frac{(h^{\prime})^{2}}{2}\}_{p}},

where

\xi\in\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{4},\,h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}/p^{-\vartheta(\xi_{3},\xi_{4})}\mathbb{Z}_{p},\,1\leq|\tau|_{p}\leq\|(\xi_{3},\xi_{4})\|_{p}.

2.4.
Notation

Throughout this section
\mathbb{G}
will denote a compact nilpotent
p
-adic Lie group with dimension
d\leq 5
. In order to study the behavior of the directional VT operators, we need to introduce first several important definitions and notation. First, we have the notation about representation theory of compact Vilenkin groups.

Definition 2.8
.

•

The symbol
\mathrm{Rep}(\mathbb{G})
will denote the collection of all (equivalence classes of) unitary finite-dimensional representations of
\mathbb{G}
. We will denote by
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
the
unitary dual of
\mathbb{G}
, that is, the collection of all equivalence classes of
\mathrm{Rep}(\mathbb{G})
.

•

Let
K
be a normal sub-group of
\mathbb{G}
. We denote by
K^{\bot}
the
anihilator of
K
, here defined as

K^{\bot}:=\{[\pi]\in\mathrm{Rep}(\mathbb{G})\,:\,\pi|_{K}=I_{d_{\pi}}\}.

Also, we will use the notation

B_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}(n):=\widehat{\mathbb{G}}\cap\mathbb{G}(p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\bot},

and
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}(n):=B_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}(n)\setminus B_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}(n-1).

Remark 2.9
.

Recall how
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{d}\cong\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}
is actually an ultrametric space with the
p
-adic norm, and the balls of
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{d}
are precisely the balls of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{d}
modulo
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}
. For
\mathbb{H}_{d}
and
\mathcal{B}_{4}
, their unitary duals can be identified with a certain subset of
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{2d+1}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{4}
respectively, and this implies the same for any compact nilpotent
p
-adic Lie group with dimension
d\leq 4
. So, at least for
d\leq 4
, we already know how

B_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}(n)=\{\pi_{\xi}\,:\,\,\|\xi\|_{p}\leq p^{n}\}=\widehat{\mathbb{G}}\cap B_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{d}}(n),

where

B_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{d}}(n):=\{\xi\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}\,\,:\,\,\|\xi\|_{p}\leq p^{n}\}=p^{-n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p},\,\,\,n\in\mathbb{N}.

This highlights the motivation for our notation, which is the fact that for
d\leq 4
the anihilators of balls in
\mathbb{G}
are balls in the unitary dual
\mathbb{G}
. Similarly, the sets
\mathbb{G}(n)
are spheres. Here in this paper we will prove the same for compact nilpotent groups with dimension
d=5
.

A particularity of compact
p
-adic Lie groups, in contrast with their real counterparts, is how their continuous representations are actually
smooth functions
. We say that a function
f:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{C}
is
smooth
if
f
is a locally constant function with a fixed index of local constancy, i.e., there is an
n_{f}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, which we always choose to be the minimum possible, such that

f(\mathbf{x}\star\textbf{y})=f(\mathbf{x}),\,\,\,\text{ for all}\,\,\,\textbf{y}\in\mathbb{G}(p^{n_{f}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}).

Here
\star
is the operation on
\mathbb{G}
. The reason why any
[\pi]\in\mathrm{Rep}(\mathbb{G})
must be smooth is that, due to their continuity and the lack of small subgroups for the general group
\mathrm{GL}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})
, any unitary representation
[\pi]:\mathbb{G}\to\mathrm{GL}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})
must map a certain ball
\mathbb{G}(p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}),n\in\mathbb{N},
to the identity element. In other words, any continuous representation of
\mathbb{G}
must have a non-trivial kernel, which is a compact open subgroup of
\mathbb{G}
. This is the reason why Definition
2.8
makes sense. We will denote by
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G})
the collection of all smooth complex-valued functions on
\mathbb{G}
, and
\mathcal{D}_{n}(\mathbb{G})
will denote the collection of smooth functions with index of local constancy equal to
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

By the Peter-Weyl theorem, one has a definition of Fourier series on general compact groups, which is given in terms of the representation theory of noncommutative groups. The matrix entries of the unitary irreducible representations span a dense subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{G})
, and we can write any
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{G})
as

f(\mathbf{x}):=\sum_{[\xi]\in\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}d_{\xi}Tr[\xi(\mathbf{x})\widehat{f}(\xi)].

A powerful implication of this series the representations is that, if a given linear operator turns out to be invariant, let’s say it commutes with left translations, then it is diagonalized by the matrix entries of the representations. And even if the operator is not that compatible with the group structure, we can still exploit the Fourier series of the group to write it down in a way that simplifies many problems. See for instance
[
26
]
, where it is shown how a densely defined linear operator on a compact group has the following symbolic representation:

Tf(\mathbf{x}):=\sum_{[\xi]\in\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}d_{\xi}Tr[\xi(\mathbf{x})\sigma_{T}(\mathbf{x},\xi)\widehat{f}(\xi)].

Here the symbol
\sigma_{T}
is a matrix valued map which we define formally below.

Definition 2.10
.

•

We will use the notation
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{G})
for the space of
smooth distributions
on
\mathbb{G}
, that is, the collection of all continuous linear functionals
F:\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G})\to\mathbb{C}
. Here
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G})
is considered a topological vectors space with the topology of convergence over finite-dimensional sub-spaces.

•

A
symbol
\sigma
is a mapping

\sigma:\mathbb{G}\times\mathrm{Rep}(\mathbb{G})\to\bigcup_{[\pi]\in\mathrm{Rep}(G)}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi}),\,\,\,\,(\mathbf{x},[\pi])\mapsto\sigma(\mathbf{x},\pi)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi}).

Given a symbol on
\mathbb{G}
, we define its associated pseudo-differential operator as the linear operator
T_{\sigma}
acting on
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G})
via the formula

T_{\sigma}f(\mathbf{x}):=\sum_{[\xi]\in\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}d_{\xi}Tr[\xi(\mathbf{x})\sigma(\mathbf{x},\xi)\widehat{f}(\xi)].

•

Conversely, given a densely defined linear operator
T:\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G})\subset D(T)\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{G})
, we define its associated symbol via the formula

\sigma(\mathbf{x},[\pi])=\pi^{*}(\mathbf{x})T\pi(\mathbf{x}),\quad[\pi]\in\mathrm{Rep}(\mathbb{G}).

Remark 2.11
.

The symbol
\sigma_{T}
of a linear operator is well defined. To see it, take
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in[\xi]\in\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
. Then there is some unitary linear mapping
M
such that
M^{*}\pi M=\pi^{\prime}
, and for that reason

\widehat{f}(\pi^{\prime})=\int_{\mathbb{G}}f(\mathbf{x})(\pi^{\prime})^{*}(\mathbf{x})d\mathbf{x}=\int_{\mathbb{G}}f(\mathbf{x})M^{*}\pi^{*}(\mathbf{x})Md\mathbf{x}=M^{*}\widehat{f}(\pi)M.

Hence, the Fourier series of a function are well defined and independent of the representative chosen for each class, because

Tr(\pi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})\widehat{f}(\pi^{\prime}))=Tr(M^{*}\pi(\mathbf{x})MM^{*}\widehat{f}(\pi)M)=Tr(M^{*}\pi(\mathbf{x})\widehat{f}(\pi)M)=Tr(\pi(\mathbf{x})\widehat{f}(\pi)).

Similarly

\sigma_{T}(\mathbf{x},\pi^{\prime})=M^{*}\pi^{*}(\mathbf{x})T\pi(\mathbf{x})M=M^{*}\sigma(\mathbf{x},\pi)M,

so we can see that

Tr(\pi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})\sigma_{T}(\mathbf{x},\pi^{\prime})\widehat{f}(\pi^{\prime}))=Tr(M^{*}\pi(\mathbf{x})\sigma_{T}(\mathbf{x},\pi)\widehat{f}(\pi)M)=Tr(\pi(\mathbf{x})\sigma_{T}(\mathbf{x},\pi)\widehat{f}(\pi)).

To conclude with this subsection, we need to define formally the concept of global hypoellipticity we will be using in the following sections. As we anticipated in the introduction, there is actually two kinds of ”smooth” functions on
\mathbb{G}
, but here we are interested only in
Schwartz functions
and the concept of global hypoellipticity attached to their definition. This property will be a great example of a wide range of features of operators which are encoded in the behavior of their symbol.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a compact
p
-adic Lie group, and let
\mathrm{Rep}(\mathbb{G})
be the collection of all unitary continuous finite-dimensional representations of
\mathbb{G}
.

•

The
Schwartz
space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
is defined as the collection of all
L^{2}
-functions such that

\|\widehat{f}(\xi)\|_{HS}\lesssim\langle\xi\rangle_{\mathbb{G}}^{-k},\,\,\,\text{for all}\,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

where
\widehat{f}(\xi):=\int_{\mathbb{G}}f(x)\xi^{*}(x)dx,
and
\langle\xi\rangle_{\mathbb{G}}^{k}
denotes the eigenvalue of the Vladimirov-Taibleson operator
\mathbb{D}^{k}
defined in Definition
2.14
, associated to the class
[\xi]\in\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
.

•

Let
T_{\sigma}:\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G})\subset D(T)\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{G})
be a densely defined linear operator. We say that
T_{\sigma}
is
globally hypoelliptic
if the condition
T_{\sigma}f=g
with
f\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{G})
and
g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
implies that
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
.

Remark 2.13
.

It is well known, see for instance
[
18
]
, how the global hypoellipticity of an invariant operator is equivalent to the condition

\langle\pi\rangle_{\mathbb{G}}^{m}\lesssim\|\sigma(\pi)\|_{inf},\,\,\,m\in\mathbb{R},\,\,\quad\text{for all}\,\,[\pi]\in\widehat{\mathbb{G}}.

Here we are using the notation

\|\sigma(\pi)\|_{inf}:=\inf\{\|\sigma(\pi)v\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}\,:\,\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}=1\}.

Here
\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}
denotes the Hilbert space norm a vector
v
in the representation space.

2.5.
VT operators

One important idea from the theory of differential and pseudo-differential operators on Lie groups, is the correspondence between directional derivatives and elements of the Lie algebra. However, in the
p
-adic case, there are plenty of non-trivial locally constant functions, due to the fact that
p
-adic numbers are totally disconnected. This means that the usual notion of derivative does not apply, and therefore we need to find an alternative kind of operators to talk about differentiability on these groups. A first approach to this problem can be the
Vladimitov-Taibleson operator

[
12
,
13
]
, which we define for general compact
\mathbb{K}
-Lie groups as follows:

Definition 2.14
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a non-archimedean local field with ring of integers
\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}
, prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}=\textbf{p}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}
and residue field
\mathbb{F}_{q}\cong\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}/\textbf{p}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}
. Let
\mathbb{G}\leq\mathrm{GL}_{m}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}})
be a compact
d
-dimensional
\mathbb{K}
-Lie group. We define the
Vladimirov–Taibleson operator
on
\mathbb{G}
via the formula

D^{\alpha}f(\mathbf{x}):=\frac{1-q^{\alpha}}{1-q^{-(\alpha+d)}}\int_{\mathbb{G}}\frac{f(\mathbf{x}\star\mathbf{y}^{-1})-f(\mathbf{x})}{\|\mathbf{y}\|_{\mathbb{K}}^{\alpha+d}}d\mathbf{y},

where

\|\mathbf{y}\|_{\mathbb{K}}:=\begin{cases}1,\,&\,\,\text{if}\,\,\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathrm{GL}_{m}(\textbf{p}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{K}}),\\
